Explicit Codes approaching Generalized Singleton Bound using Expanders
Fernando Granha Jeronimo, Tushant Mittal, Shashank Srivastava, Madhur Tulsiani

Abstract
We construct a new family of explicit codes that are list decodable to capacity and achieve an optimal list size of O ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) O(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\varepsilon}}) . In contrast to existing explicit constructions of codes achieving list decoding capacity, our arguments do not rely on algebraic structure but utilize simple combinatorial properties of expander graphs. Our construction is based on a celebrated distance amplification procedure due to Alon, Edmonds, and Luby [FOCS’95], which transforms any high-rate code into one with near-optimal rate-distance tradeoff. We generalize it to show that the same procedure can be used to transform any high-rate code into one that achieves list decoding capacity. Our proof can be interpreted as a “local-to-global” phenomenon for (a slight strengthening of) the generalized Singleton bound. Using this construction, for every R , ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) R,\varepsilon\in(0,1) and k ∈ ℕ + k\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+} , we obtain an explicit family of codes 𝒞 ⊆ Σ n \mathcal{C}\subseteq\Sigma^{n} , with rate R R such that, • They achieve the ε \varepsilon -relaxed generalized Singleton bound: for any g ∈ Σ n g\in\Sigma^{n} and any list ℋ {\mathcal{H}} of at most k k codewords, we have, 𝔼 h ∈ ℋ ​ [ Δ ⁡ ( g , h ) ] ≥ | ℋ | − 1 | ℋ | ⋅ ( 1 − R − ε ) . \mathchoice{\underset{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}~\geq~\frac{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1}{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert}\cdot(1-R-\varepsilon)\,. • The alphabet size is a constant depending only on ε \varepsilon and k k . • They can be list decoded up to radius k − 1 k ​ ( 1 − R − ε ) \frac{k-1}{k}(1-R-\varepsilon) , in time n O k , ε ​ ( 1 ) n^{O_{k,\varepsilon}(1)} . As a corollary of our result, we also obtain the first explicit construction of LDPC codes achieving list decoding capacity, and in fact arbitrarily close to the generalized Singleton bound.



1
Introduction

An error-correcting code
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
is a collection of strings (codewords) over a finite alphabet
\Sigma
, such that every pair of codewords is well separated.
Two important parameters associated with a code
{\mathcal{C}}
are its distance
\delta({\mathcal{C}})
and rate
\rho({\mathcal{C}})
, which measure respectively the worst-case error correction capability of a code, and the amount of redundancy present in the code. These are defined as

\delta({\mathcal{C}})~=~\min_{\underset{f\neq g}{f,g\in{\mathcal{C}}}}\frac{1}{n}\cdot\lvert\left\{i\in[n]~|~f_{i}\neq g_{i}\right\}\rvert\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho({\mathcal{C}})~:=~\frac{1}{n}\cdot\log_{\lvert\Sigma\rvert}\lvert{\mathcal{C}}\rvert\,.

As may be expected, the separation between codewords (distance) involves a tradeoff with the number of codewords (rate). This is captuted by the well-known Singleton bound, (the asymptotic version of) which implies that for any code
{\mathcal{C}}
, we must have
\delta({\mathcal{C}})\leq 1-\rho({\mathcal{C}})
.
The study of the achievable tradeoffs between rate
\rho
and distance
\delta
is a fundamental problem in the theory of error-correcting codes, with the tradeoffs given by the Singleton bound being optimal in the regime of large alphabets
\Sigma
.

Several code families are known to achieve the Singleton bound including the celebrated Reed-Solomon codes
[
RS60
]
, multiplicity codes
[
KSY14
,
Kop15
]
, and many others
[
GRS23
]
.
While the above codes achieving the Singleton bound often require an alphabet size growing with
n
, a particularly relevant construction by Alon, Edmonds, and Luby
[
AEL95
]
also yields near-optimal tradeoffs with a
constant
alphabet size.
In particular, this construction gives
\delta\geq 1-\rho-\varepsilon
using an alphabet of size
2^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
.

Moreover, the above code constructions with near-optimal distances also enable highly efficient algorithms for the task of unique decoding, which is the task of recovering a codeword uniquely from, say a
\beta
fraction of adversarial errors.
For a code with distance
\delta
, we must have
\beta<\delta/2
, and it is indeed possible to achieve this algorithmically using the structure of several code constructions above.
However, unique recovery is no longer possible when
\beta\geq\delta/2
, since the corruptions may be consistent with more than one codewords.

List decoding capacity.

The notion of list decoding defined by Elias
[
Eli57
]
and Wozencraft
[
Woz58
]
allows the output to be a small
list
of codewords, when the fraction of errors is higher than the unique-decoding radius of
\delta/2
.
In this setting, both the
combinatorial
question of bounding the number of codewords in the list, and the
algorithmic
one of finding the list, are quite non-trivial and have been the subject of extensive research. Algorithms for list decoding were considered in the foundational works of Guruswami and Sudan
[
Sud97
,
GS98
]
who gave the first list decoding algorithms for decoding Reed-Solomon codes up to a threshold known as the Johnson bound, where the list size is known to be bounded for any code.
Since then, there has been tremendous progress on both the combinatorial and algorithmic aspects of list decoding, and we refer the reader to excellent references by Guruswami, Rudra, and Sudan
[
Gur06
,
GRS23
]
for more details.
The constructions and algorithms for list-decodable codes have also had numerous applications in the areas of complexity theory
[
Sud00
,
Tre04
]
and pseudorandomness
[
GUV09
,
Vad12
]
, leading to several deep and beautiful connections between these areas.

In terms of combinatorial bounds for list decoding
beyond
the Johnson bound, volume arguments can be used to show that for the lists to be subexponential in
n
, we must have
\rho\leq 1-H_{q}(\beta)
, where
q=\lvert\Sigma\rvert
and
H_{q}(x)
denotes the
q
-ary entropy function (which approximately equals
x
for large
q
). This bound is called the list decoding capacity, and is known to be achievable using random codes
[
GRS23
]
and random linear codes
[
GHK11
]
.

A breakthrough result of Guruswami and Rudra
[
GR06
,
GR08
]
, building on the work of Parvaresh and Vardy
[
PV05
]
, gave the first explicit construction of codes achieving list decoding capacity.
Their construction was based on folded Reed-Solomon codes, and yielded a list of size
n^{O(1/\varepsilon)}
up to an error radius of
1-\rho-\varepsilon
, using an alphabet of size
n^{O(1/\varepsilon^{2})}
.
Since then, there has been a series of exciting works on reducing the list size and alphabet size, combining folded Reed-Solomon codes with pseudorandom objects like
subspace evasive sets

[
Gur11
,
DL12
,
GW13
]
, expanders
[
KRSW18
]
and
subspace designs

[
GK16
,
GRZ21
]
.
A series of recent works have also led to significantly better bounds on the list sizes for folded Reed-Solomon codes via a deeper understanding of their structure, starting with beautiful work of Kopparty et al.
[
KRSW18
,
KRZSW23
]
and Tamo
[
Tam24
]
yielding list sizes
(1/\varepsilon)^{O(1/\varepsilon)}
, followed by a bound of
O(1/\varepsilon^{2})
by Srivastava
[
Sri25
]
, and culminating in an asymptotically optimal bound of
O(1/\varepsilon)
by Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
.

In addition to folded Reed-Solomon codes and their variants, there has also been several other explicit constructions of codes achieving list decoding capacity using algebraic-geometric codes
[
Gur09
,
GX13
,
GRZ21
,
GX22
]
, multiplicity codes
[
Kop15
]
, polynomial ideal codes
[
BHKS23
]
and subcodes of Reed-Solomon codes
[
GX13
,
BST24
]
.
The algebraic constructions above also come with efficient list decoding algorithms using polynomial interpolation methods, which have been a cornerstone of techniques in the area of list decoding.
However, the explicit construction known to achieve a list size of
O(1/\varepsilon)
at radii
\varepsilon
-close to list decoding capacity, are folded Reed-solomon and multiplicity codes, via the very recent result of Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
. In fact, they show that these codes which in fact achieve two additional strengthenings of the notion of list decoding, as discussed below.

Average-radius list decoding.

A stronger form of combinatorial list decoding was considered by Guruswami and Narayanan
[
GN14
]
, which shows that the list size at radius
\beta
is bounded by
k
by considering a strengthening of the contrapositive.
Note that the bound on the list size is true if for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we have
\max_{i\in[k]}\Delta(g,h_{i})>\beta
, where
\Delta(g,h)
denotes the fractional Hamming distance as before.
The notion of average-radius list decoding requires establishing that in fact
\mathchoice{\underset{i\in[k]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}>\beta
which also bounds the list size for a stronger reason.

Starting with the beautiful works of Rudra and Wootters
[
Woo13
,
RW14
]
which introduced techniques from high-dimensional probability, there have been a sequence of results that random linear codes, and random puncturings of structured codes, can in fact achieve list decoding capacity
[
FKS22
,
GST23
,
GLS+24
]
, with several of the results even applicable for the average-radius list decoding
[
BGM23
,
GZ23
,
AGL24b
]
.

Generalized Singleton bounds.

Shangguan and Tamo
[
ST20
]
(and independently Roth
[
Rot22
]
) considered a generalization of the Singleton bound in the context of combinatorial list decoding for Reed-Solomon codes, showing (in the asymptotic version) that for any code
{\mathcal{C}}
, if the list size at error-radius
\beta
is always bounded by
k-1
, then one must have
\beta\leq\frac{k-1}{k}\cdot(1-\rho({\mathcal{C}}))
.
While
k=2
recovers the Singleton bound on the unique-decoding radius and hence the distance, the above bound also shows that lists at radius
1-\rho-\varepsilon
must have size
(1-\rho-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon
. which they conjectured to be optimal.
The generalized Singleton bound also gives a different strengthening of the notion of list decoding, this time with a more fine-grained tradeoff for every list size.

Shangguan and Tamo
[
ST20
]
also give an explicit construction of a Reed-Solomon code achieving the generalized Singleton bound for
k=3
, with alphabet size
q=2^{2^{n}}
.
Recent works on random puncturings of Reed-Solomon and related codes show that these in fact achieve the
average-distance version
of (a relaxed) generalized Singleton bound, satisfying for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

\mathchoice{\underset{i\in[k]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[k]}\left[\Delta(g,h_{i})\right]}~\geq~\frac{k-1}{k}\cdot(1-\rho-\varepsilon)\,.

In a sequence of works, the alphabet size requirement for the above random constructions has been brought down from exponential in
n

[
BGM23
]
to polynomial
[
GZ23
]
to linear in
n

[
AGL24b
]
.
It was also shown by
[
BDG24
]
that exponential alphabet size is
necessary
for the exact version of the Singleton bound, and thus, the relaxation of the bound by
\varepsilon
is required for smaller alphabet sizes.
It was also proved by Alrabiah, Guruswami, and Li
[
AGL24b
]
that random linear codes with alphabet size
2^{1/\varepsilon^{O(1)}}
can achieve the average-radius relaxed generalized Singleton bound for each fixed
k
, and that a dependence of
2^{O_{k}(1/\varepsilon)}

[
AGL24a
]
is necessary even for the relaxed version.

Very recently, Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
proved that folded Reed-Solomon codes satisfy the average-radius (relaxed) generalized Singleton bound.
Their construction is explicit and algorithmic, and achieves the
\varepsilon
-relaxed bound with alphabet size
n^{O(k/\varepsilon)}
.

1.1
The Alon-Edmonds-Luby (AEL) construction and our results

We give an explicit construction of codes satisfying the average-radius (
\varepsilon
-relaxed)
generalized Singleton bound, for every fixed list size
k-1
, with a constant alphabet size
depending only on
\varepsilon
and
k
(we denote the list size by
k-1
since it makes it easier to state the average-distance inequality for
k
points).
Our codes also satisfy the low-density parity check (LDPC) property i.e., they can described as kernels of sparse (parity-check) matrices with constant sparsity in each row. While random LDPC codes are known to achieve list decoding capacity
[
MRRZ+20
]
, no explicit constructions of such codes were previously known.

1.1
Informal version of
Corollary

3.7
.

For every
\rho,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist an
explicit
family of codes
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
, such that
\rho({\mathcal{C}})\geq\rho
and
for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
and any
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
with
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert\leq k

\mathchoice{\underset{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}~\geq~\frac{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1}{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert}\cdot(1-\rho-\varepsilon)\,.

Moreover, the code
\mathcal{C}
has alphabet size
2^{(k^{k}/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}
and is characterized by parity checks of size
O((k^{k}/\varepsilon)^{O(1)})
.

By choosing
k=O(1/\varepsilon)
in the above theorem, we also get codes which are average-radius list
decodable up to capacity, with the list size at error radius
1-\rho-\varepsilon
bounded by
O(1/\varepsilon)
.

1.2
.

For every
\rho,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there exist an
explicit
family of codes
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
, such that
\rho({\mathcal{C}})\geq\rho
and
for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
, the list
{\mathcal{L}}(g,1-\rho-\varepsilon)
at radius
1-\rho-\varepsilon
satisfies

\lvert{\mathcal{L}}(g,1-\rho-\varepsilon)\rvert~=~\lvert\left\{h\in{\mathcal{C}}~\mid~\Delta(g,h)\leq 1-\rho-\varepsilon\right\}\rvert~=~O(1/\varepsilon)\,.

Moreover, the code
\mathcal{C}
has alphabet size
2^{2^{(1/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}}
and is characterized by parity checks of size
2^{(1/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}
.

Our construction is elementary in nature, and relies on a celebrated distance amplification technique of Alon, Edmonds, and Luby
[
AEL95
]
(AEL) based on expander graphs.
We derive the above theorem as a consequence of a more general result about a “local-to-global” amplification for average-radius list decodability via the AEL construction.
We note that in contrast to previous algebraic constructions of codes achieving list decoding
capacity, our arguments do not need to rely on polynomial interpolation or other algebraic
phenomena, and can be proved using simple combinatorial and spectral properties of expander graphs.

Before describing our results in detail, we recall how the AEL construction can be used to produce an infinite family of codes arbitrarily close to the Singleton bound for distance.

The AEL construction.

Given a balanced
d
-regular bipartitite graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
\lvert L\rvert=\lvert R\rvert=n
, we call it an
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander if the second singular value of the (normalized) biadjacency matrix is at most
\lambda
.

L
R
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\ni\big\lparen f(e_{1}),f(e_{1}),f(e_{3})\big\rparen=f_{\ell}
f_{r}=\Big\lparen f(e_{2}),f(e_{4}),f(e_{5})\Big\rparen\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
f(e_{1})
f(e_{2})
f(e_{3})
f(e_{4})
f(e_{5})

Figure 1:
Illustration of the AEL procedure

The AEL construction works with such an expanding graph, an “inner code”
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
and an “outer code”
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^{n}
, with
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
.
We view a codeword of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
as being written on the left vertices (formally,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^{L}
. The symbol at each vertex
\ell\in L
is then viewed as an element of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
, which gives a labeling of the edges (with an ordering fixed in advance) and thus an element of
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{E}
.
Each right vertex
r\in R
the
n collects the
d
symbols from the incident edges, forming a symbol in
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
.
The procedure can be viewed as using
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
to map
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
to a new code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}\subseteq(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{R}\cong(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{n}
.
As illustrated in
Fig.

1
, we can think of symbols being on the edges of
G
, which form a codeword in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
when grouped from the right. We will need to consider both the fraction of left and right vertices in which two codewords differ, and will denote these distances using
\Delta_{L}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\Delta_{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
respectively.

An easy computation shows that
\rho({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})\geq\rho({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}})\cdot\rho({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})
. Moreover, an application of the expander mixing lemma can be used to show that

\delta({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})~\geq~\delta({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})-\frac{\lambda}{\delta({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}})}\,.

Thus, when
\rho({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}})\geq 1-\varepsilon
and
\lambda\leq\varepsilon\cdot\delta({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}})
, this gives a “local-to-global” amplification of the rate-distance tradeoffs obtained by the code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
: we get
\rho({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})\geq\rho({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})-\varepsilon
and
\delta({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})\geq\delta({\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})-\varepsilon
.
In particular, instantiating the construction with a “constant-sized” code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
achieving the Singleton bound, yields an infinite family of codes (corresponding to a family of expander graphs) which achieve a tradeoff
\varepsilon
-close to the Singleton bound.

In addition to giving a local-to-global amplification, the AEL procedure is also highly versatile and has been adapted for a number of applications, particularly because it also preserves structural properties of the outer code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
.
It is easy to see
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
are both linear over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, then so is the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
.
It also preserves the property of being LDPC, being locally testable or locally correctable
[
GKO+17
,
KMRZS16
]
, linear time unique decodability
[
GI05
]
, and also the duality properties needed for quantum codes
[
BGG24
]
.

Hemenway and Wootters
[
HW18
]
also used the AEL procedure to construct linear-time decodable codes achieving list decoding capacity for
erasures
. We refer the reader to the excellent discussion in
[
KMRZS16
]
on the various applications of this procedure.

Local-to-global results for generalized Singleton bound.

In the subsequent discussion, we will only consider relaxed versions of the generalized Singleton bound (and will not mention the qualifier explicitly), since it is necessary for having alphabet sizes to be subexponential in
n
.
Similar to the rate-distance tradeoffs discussed above, we show that the AEL procedure also gives a “local-to-global” amplification for (a slight strengthening of) the property of satisfying the average-radius generalized Singleton bound.

For a code
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
with distance
\delta
, and
S\subseteq n
with
\lvert S\rvert/n=s
, considered the “erased” code
{\mathcal{C}}_{S}
, where all coordinates in
S
are replaced by a special symbol
\bot
and thus do not contribute to distances. The relative distance of the resulting code (which still has
n
coordinates) is at least
\delta-s
.
If the erased code
{\mathcal{C}}_{S}
is list decodable with list size
k
at error radius
\beta=(k/(k+1))\cdot(\delta-s-\varepsilon)
, we would have for any
g
, and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k+1}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{S}

\sum_{i\in[k+1]}\Delta(g,h_{i})~\geq~k\cdot(\delta-s-\varepsilon)\,.

We say that the code
(\delta,k_{0},\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable
with erasures
if the above inequality is true for
all erased codes

{\mathcal{C}}_{S}
and all
k<k_{0}
.
Note that this property can equivalently be stated by simply erasing symbols in coordinates from
S
in the center
g\in\Sigma^{n}
to form the erased word
\overline{g}\in(\Sigma\cup\{\bot\})^{n}
and not counting the
\bot
symbols towards distances from codewords in
{\mathcal{C}}
(which is how we define it in
Definition

3.1
).
We show that the AEL procedure gives a local-to-global amplification for the above property.

1.3
Restatement of
Theorem

3.2
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer and let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be a
code obtained using the AEL construction using
(G,{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}},{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})
, where
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
is
(\delta_{0},k,\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, and
G
is a
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander for
\lambda\leq\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{out}}}{6{k}^{k}}\cdot\varepsilon
.
Then,
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
(\delta_{0},k,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures.

A reader may notice that instead of the erased codes
{\mathcal{C}}_{S}
, we could have equivalently considered the punctured codes obtained by
restricting
to coordinates in
\overline{S}
, which would yield the same inequality up to normalization factors of
1-s
.
This view can be used to show that several known results for random codes and random linear codes also yield average radius list-decodability with erasures (via a simple union bound over puncturings).
This proves the existence of the inner codes
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, satisfying the necessary conditions of
Theorem

1.3
with
\delta_{0}=1-\rho
, which can then be used to construct codes
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
satisfying the generalized Singleton bound.
Moreover, if one wants even the inner codes to be fully explicit, they can also be chosen to be folded Reed-Solomon codes, using the results of Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
.
Instantiating
Theorem

1.3
with any of the above choices for
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, and appropriate families of graphs
G
and codes
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
, immediately yields the family of codes promised in
Theorem

1.1
.

Remarks.

Given the versatility of the AEL framework, we can also obtain additional structural properties for the codes resulting from our constructions.

-

As discussed earlier, if both
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
are
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear, then so
is the resulting code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
. Note that the alphabet size for
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
q^{d}
and thus, we
obtain linearity only over a
subfield
, as is also the case for other capacity-achieving
codes such as folded Reed-Solomon.

-

Our construction only needs the outer code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
to have high rate and (arbitrarily small) constant distance. In particular, we do not need the outer code to be list decodable for our structural or algorithmic results. Moreover, starting from outer codes with additional properties (such as being LDPC), the AEL construction can often inherit these properties.

-

If the code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
is LDPC with parity checks of size at most
w
, then
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is LDPC with parity checks of size at most
w\cdot d

over the subfield

{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.
Instantiating with an LDPC code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
, we thus also get an explicit construction of LDPC codes achieving list decoding capacity, with parity checks of size
2^{(1/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}
, where
\varepsilon
is the (arbitrarily small) gap to capacity. Examples of such codes were only known via random constructions
[
MRRZ+20
]
.

-

We note that while we state our results in terms of the average-radius generalized Singleton bound, the AEL construction can also be used to obtain local-to-global results directly for list decoding capacity
[
Sri24
]
, relying only on a bound on the list size for the inner code.
However, the statement in terms of the average-radius bounds leads to a simpler proof and stronger guarantees, and also directly leads to list decoding algorithms via the “proofs to algorithms”
paradigm
[
FKP19
]
.

Algorithms.

The proofs for our theorems rely on simple spectral inequalities for expander graphs, which can then
be turned into decoding algorithms, using the Sum-of-Squares (SoS) hierarchy of semidefinite
programs.
Our algorithms rely on the “proofs to algorithms” framework
[
FKP19
]
adapted to the setting of codes
[
JST23
]
.
This framework was used in
[
JST23
]
to obtain list decoding algorithms for AEL codes, and in
this work we show how this can be extended to obtain list decoding algorithms matching the
generalized Singleton bound.

1.4
Informal version of
Corollary

5.25
.

For every
\rho,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist an
explicit
family of codes
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
, such that
\rho({\mathcal{C}})\geq\rho
and
for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
and any
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
with
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert\leq k

\mathchoice{\underset{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\left[\Delta(g,h)\right]}~\geq~\frac{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1}{\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert}\cdot(1-\rho-\varepsilon)\,.

The code
\mathcal{C}
has alphabet size
2^{(k^{k}/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}
, and can be list decoded from
radius
\frac{k-1}{k}(1-\rho-\varepsilon)
deterministically in time
n^{2^{(k^{k}/\varepsilon)^{O(1)}}}
with a
list of size at most
k-1
.

Broadly speaking, the SoS framework requires one to prove properties of an object by writing the
object as a set of formal variables, and expressing any desired inequalities as sums-of-squares of
low-degree polynomials in these formal variables.
The properties of the SoS hierarchy then allow one to write a convex relaxation to search for such an object,
and to deduce these properties also hold for “convex proxy” for the object obtained as a solution
to the relaxation.

We obtain the above theorem, by reasoning about a convex proxy for (real embeddings of) the entire list
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\right\}
, replacing codewords by vectors of formal variables
\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\right\}
.
The spectral expansion of a graph
G
can be expressed in the form
A_{G}-u_{0}u_{0}^{\intercal}\preceq\lambda\cdot I
, where
A_{G}
is the normalized adjacency matrix and
u_{0}
is the
(known) top eigenvector.
A spectral inequality relying on expansion can often be reduced to understanding a quadratic form

\left\langle{\mathbf{Z}},(\lambda\cdot I-A_{G}+u_{0}u_{0}^{\intercal}){\mathbf{Z}}\right\rangle
which can be viewed as a sum-of-squares
expression in the variables
{\mathbf{Z}}
since the quadratic form
\left\langle{\mathbf{Z}},M{\mathbf{Z}}\right\rangle
of a positive
semidefinite matrix
M=\sum_{\sigma_{i}}v_{i}v_{i}^{\intercal}
is
\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\left\langle v_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}\right\rangle^{2}
.

The key contribution of our work is the “proof” part of this
proofs-to-algorithms implementation.
Adapting the framework from
[
JST23
]
to our setting does require some nontrivial technical ideas (discussed in the proof overview
below) but a reader interested in understanding the code construction and properties can safely skip the algorithmic aspects of our result (everything after
Section

4
) on a first reading.

1.2
An overview of proofs and techniques

We now give a brief overview of the proof of
Theorem

1.3
.
Consider
h\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
and
g\in(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{n}
, where we measure the distance

\Delta_{R}(g,h)
as the fraction of right vertices
r\in R
on which
g
and
h
differ.
Since the goal is to deduce inequalities about “global” distances of the form
\Delta_{R}(g,h)

using “local” inequalities for the inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, we also consider the local views

g
and
h
from each vertex
\ell\in L
.
Formally, we can also view
g,h\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{E}
as labeling the edges, which defines for each

\ell\in L
local views
g_{\ell},h_{\ell}\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
. For
g_{\ell}
and
h_{\ell}
we meaure local
distances
\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})
as a fraction of
d
edges incident on
\ell
.

Recall that the local distance inequalities show that for any
\ell\in L
,
g_{\ell}\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
and distinct
h_{1,\ell},\ldots,h_{k,\ell}

\sum_{i\in[k]}\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)\,,

and our goal is to deduce a similar inequality for the global distances (possibly with
\varepsilon^{\prime}
instead of

\varepsilon
).
Also, note that by taking

k=2
and
g_{\ell}=h_{1,\ell}
, we also get that
\Delta(h_{1,\ell},h_{2,\ell})\geq\delta_{0}-\varepsilon
, and we can treat
\delta_{0}
essentially as the distance of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, and by the AEL
bound, also for the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
.
In the argument below, we will treat
\varepsilon
as an arbitrarily small error.

Sampling bound.

A first observation, which is basically the distance proof of AEL, is that the local distances

for most

\ell\in L
provide a lower bound on the global distances.
Indeed, let
\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})\geq\theta
for a set
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
of size (say)
\varepsilon\cdot n
.
Each
\ell\in L^{\prime}
thus has
\theta\cdot d
“error edges” incident to it, on which
g_{\ell}
and

h_{\ell}
don’t match.
Let
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
be the right endpoints of these error edges and note that each
r\in R^{\prime}
must be a vertex
where
g,h
differ.
The expander mixing lemma (
Lemma

2.2
) then shows these that large (greater than
\theta
)
local distances even at
\varepsilon
fraction of
\ell\in L
imply large global distance

\lvert L^{\prime}\rvert\cdot(\theta\cdot d)~\leq~\lvert E(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})\rvert~\leq~\frac{d}{n}\cdot\lvert L^{\prime}\rvert\cdot\lvert R^{\prime}\rvert+\lambda\cdot n\cdot d\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Delta_{R}(g,h)~\geq~\frac{\lvert R^{\prime}\rvert}{n}~\geq~\theta-\lambda\cdot\frac{n}{\lvert L^{\prime}\rvert}\,.

By contrapositive, for (say)
\lambda\leq\varepsilon^{2}
, we have
\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h)+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h)+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h)+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h)+\varepsilon\right]}\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
For a given
g\in(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{R}
and codewords
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
and, we can say
using a union bound that

\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\forall i\in[k]~~\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\forall i\in[k]~~\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\forall i\in[k]~~\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})+\varepsilon\right]}{{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell\in L}\left[\forall i\in[k]~~\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})\leq\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})+\varepsilon\right]}~\geq~1-k\varepsilon\,.

A wrong proof.

Applying the sampling bound for any “good”
\ell
where all local distances
\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})

lower bound the global distances
\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})
, one could guess that we are
already done, since
g_{\ell}\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
and the local codewords
h_{1,\ell},\ldots,h_{k,\ell}
satisfy

\sum_{i\in[k]}\Delta(g_{\ell},h_{i,\ell})~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)~\Rightarrow~\sum_{i\in[k]}\Delta_{R}(g,h_{i})~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)-k\cdot\varepsilon

Of course the “bug” in the above proof is that the local projections
h_{1,\ell},\ldots,h_{k,\ell}
are not necessarily distinct even if the codewords
h_{1},\ldots,h_{\ell}
are, and thus we
cannot apply a local distance inequality for
k

distinct
codewords.
However, we can get around this issue by carefully examining the consequences of the local
projections being equal for two codewords.

Partitions and erasures.

We can think of each left vertex as inducing a partition
\tau_{\ell}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p_{\ell}})
of the set
{\mathcal{H}}
of codewords, where
h,h^{\prime}
are in the
same part if and only if
h_{\ell}=h_{\ell}^{\prime}
.
Since the total number of partitions is at most
k^{k}
, we fix a
partition
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
which occurs for at least
k^{-k}
fraction of

\ell\in L
, forming (say) the set
L^{*}
, which we still think of as somewhat large if

\lambda\ll k^{-k}
.

Consider the special case when
\tau^{*}=(\{h_{1}\},\ldots,\{h_{k-2}\},\{h_{k-1},h_{k}\})
i.e., for all
\ell\in L^{*}
, it is only the last two codewords that have the same local projection (note
that the projections still depend on
\ell
, just the partition is fixed).
Let
\{f_{1,\ell},\ldots,f_{k-1,\ell}\}
denote the set of local projections (for
\ell\in L^{*}
)
where we have
f_{k-1,\ell}=h_{k-1,\ell}=h_{k,\ell}
for all
\ell\in L^{*}
.
The local distance inequality then gives

\sum_{i\in[k-1]}\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{i,\ell})~\geq~(k-2)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)~\Rightarrow~\sum_{i\in[k-2]}\Delta_{R}(g_{\ell},h_{i})+\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{k-1,\ell})~\geq~(k-2)\cdot(\delta_{0}-2\varepsilon)

They key difference is now we no longer think of the term
\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{k-1,\ell})
as a lower bound
on
\Delta(g,h_{k-1,\ell})
and
\Delta(g,h_{k,\ell})
, but on the fraction of positions where both

h_{k-1}
and
h_{k}

simultaneuosly
differ from
g
.
This can again be seen by applying the expander mixing lemma argument above to the set

L^{\prime}=\{\ell\in L~|~\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{k-1,\ell})\geq\theta\}
.
Each
\ell\in L^{\prime}
then has
\theta\cdot d

“simultaneous error edges”, with the right endpoints being error locations for both
h_{k-1}
and
h_{k}
.

Now consider the “erased code”
{\mathcal{C}}_{S}^{\mathrm{AEL}}
where we erase the set
S\subseteq R
of size (say)
s\cdot n
of locations where
g_{r}\neq h_{k-1,r}
and
g_{r}\neq h_{k,r}
, and don’t count the
erased locations towards the distance.
Since the distance of
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is at least
\delta_{0}-\varepsilon
, the distance of
{\mathcal{C}}_{S}^{\mathrm{AEL}}
is at least

\delta_{0}-s-\varepsilon
. Also,
\Delta_{S}(g,h_{k-1})=\Delta_{R}(g,h_{k-1})-s
and similarly for

h_{k}
, since they both do differ from
g
in the erased locations. Triangle inequality then gives

\Delta_{S}(g,h_{k-1})+\Delta_{S}(g,h_{k})~\geq~\delta_{0}-s~\Rightarrow~\Delta_{R}(g,h_{k-1})+\Delta_{R}(g,h_{k})~\geq~\delta_{0}-\varepsilon+s

Adding the two inequalities above, and using the sampling lower bound
\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{k-1,\ell})\leq s+\varepsilon
, then proves the average distance inequality for
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
.

Figure 2:
Local projections and partitions

Induction.

The above idea of using erasures for common error locations, can also be extended to arbitrary
partitions
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
.
We now apply the local distance inequalities for codewords
f_{1,\ell},\ldots,f_{p,\ell}
corresponding to the different parts, at a “good” vertex
\ell\in L^{*}
where the sampling bound holds. This gives

\sum_{j\in[p]}\Delta(g_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})~\geq~(p-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\sum_{j\in[p]}s_{j}~\geq~(p-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)-p\varepsilon\,,

where
s_{j}
now denotes the fraction of simultaneous errors for all
h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
.

For each part
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
we also consider an “erased” code with
s_{j}
fraction of erasures, corresponding to common error locations of all codewords in
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
. When
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert>2
, the triangle inequality needs to be replaced by an average distance inequality (for global codewords in) the erased code.
To arrange this, we prove our results by induction on
k
, which gives that (assuming
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert\leq k-1
for all parts),

\forall j\in[p]\quad\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}(g,h)~\geq~\left(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert-1\right)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+s_{j}\,.

Adding the above inequalities and the lower bound on
\sum_{j}s_{j}
then yields the result, since
\sum_{j}\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert=k
. (Actually, we need a slight strenghtening of the inequalities above to correctly carry out the induction step, for which the details can be found in
Section

3
.)

Finally, we also need to rule out the trivial partition with only one part of size
k
, since we needed
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert\leq k-1
for all
j\in[p]
to use induction.
However, this cannot happen as this would mean that
h_{1,\ell}=\cdots=h_{k,\ell}
for (essentially) all
\ell
. But
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
are distinct codewords in
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
and thus their
projections must differ in at least
\delta_{\mathrm{out}}
fraction of left vertices.

Discussion.

As mentioned earlier, our proof techniques rely on elementary combinatorial and spectral arguments,
instead of polynomial interpolation, which has been the workhorse for list size bounds in all
previous explicit constructions achieving list decoding capacity.
As compared to previous applications of AEL for list decoding, the key difference here is a
fine-grained analysis of the structure of the local lists.
While many previous analyses treated the local lists as a black box and relied on stronger (list
recovery) properties of the outer code for algorithmic applications, the idea of keeping track of
their structure, such as in terms of common error locations, may also be useful for other applications.

Connections to interleaved codes.

The idea of using error locations of some parts of a code as erasures for the remaining code was already used in the work of Gopalan, Guruswami, and Raghavendra
[
GGR09
]
. However, there the focus was to show that when starting with an already good list decodable code, its list size does not degrade too much upon interleaving. The interleaving operation can be seen as the dense analog of AEL, but since it does not change the blocklength, the results in
[
GGR09
]
did not give an infinite family of codes starting from a single (inner) code as we do.

In more detail, an order-
t
interleaving of a code
{\mathcal{C}}
of blocklength
n
can be seen as applying the AEL procedure with
{\mathcal{C}}
as the inner code and
{\mathcal{C}}^{t}
as the trivial outer code, on the complete bipartite graph
K(t,n)
. By replacing all uses of the expander mixing lemma in the proof by
E(S,T)=|S|\cdot|T|
(or equivalently,
\lambda=0
), one can verify that if the code
{\mathcal{C}}
achieves generalized Singleton bound with erasures, so does its order-
t
interleaving. This also improves upon the list size that one could obtain via a black-box application of
[
GGR09
]
on a code that lies close to the generalized Singleton bound.

The idea of using error locations as erasures can also be used to prove weak non-trivial bounds on list size of interleaved codes in general, and follows the same structure as the proof of Schwartz-Zippel lemma. In particular, both the list codewords for the interleaving of
arbitrary
codes and the roots of a multivariate polynomial are contained in sets with a common structure, which immediately gives list size upper bounds. Such results were known before for the interleaving of Reed-Solomon codes, which can be derived using multivariate interpolation combined with the Schwartz-Zippel lemma
[
CS03
,
PV05
,
GX13
]
. Our argument using erasures is however applicable to general codes and uses only on their distance, which is surprising because for non-algebraic codes the analog of multivariate interpolation is not clear at all. We believe this combinatorial handle on interleaved codes sheds light on interpolation-based decoders, and may find further applications. The interested reader is referred to
[
Sri24
]
for details.

Outline of our algorithm.

As discussed earlier, our algorithm for list decoding a given
g\in\Sigma^{n}
searches for “convex
proxies” given by the SoS hierarchy, for the entire list of codewords in a ball around
g
.
We consider vectors
{\mathbf{Z}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}
corresponding to (real embeddings of) the codewords
and the solutions to the SoS relaxation can be described as objects
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]},\ldots,\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\right]}
containing values corresponding to all low-degree monomials in the variables,
which we refer to as “pseudocodewords”.
One can also extend
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
by linearity to form a linear operator which yields values for
all polynomials. The constraints of the SoS hierarchy imply that
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[P({\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[P({\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[P({\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[P({\mathbf{Z}})^{2}\right]}\geq 0
for all
low-degree polynomials
P
, and thus all inequalities that can be expressed as sums-of-squares of
low-degree polynomials are satisfied by the solution.

Our algorithm simply searches for the largest
k^{\prime}
for which there exists a tuple
(\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]},\ldots,\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]})
of pseudocodewords satisfying two conditions:

1.

For all
i\neq j
,
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}_{j}
are far (just as codewords are):

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{j})\right]}\geq\delta^{\prime}
.

2.

For all
i
,
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}
is within the required error radius of
g
(just as list elements are):

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}\leq\beta
.

Note that we use above that the distance
\Delta_{L}
and
\Delta_{R}
can be expressed as low-degree
polynomials in the variables
\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\right\}_{i\in[k^{\prime}]}
(which is easy diffcult to show).
The analysis for the algorithm now consists of two parts.

We first show that for a maximum
k^{\prime}
where the convex relaxation for
(\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{1}\right]},\ldots,\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}}\right]})
is feasible, but the one for
k^{\prime}+1
codewords is not, any
true
codeword

h\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
with
\Delta_{R}(g,h)\leq\beta
must (essentially) satisfy
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},h)\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},h)\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},h)\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},h)\right]}\leq\delta^{\prime}
for some
i\in[k]
.
This is because if
h
is far from all
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}
s, we can augment the solution to a
k^{\prime}+1
tuple by
adding (low-degree monomials in)
h
as
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}+1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}+1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}+1}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{k^{\prime}+1}\right]}
.
Since SoS is a relaxation, true codewords are also valid pseudocodewords, and thus
k^{\prime}
cannot
be maximum.
Given this “covering” property for the maximum
k^{\prime}
and by choosing
\delta^{\prime}
small enough, we can
obtain any
h
in the list by “unique-decoding” the corresponding codeword of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
from
the vector
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\right]}
for some
i\in[k]
.
This argument is presented in
Section

5.3
.

The second part of the proof consists in showing that
there exists a maximum

k^{\prime}
.
This follows from extending the average distance inequality to pseudocodewords, using the fact that
the proof can be expressed as sums-of-squares, and is proved in
Section

5.2
.
Formally, we get that for any
k^{\prime}
-tuple of pseudocodewords as above, we must have

\sum_{i\in[k^{\prime}]}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}~\geq~(k^{\prime}-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)\,.

However, for
\beta\leq((k-1)/k)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)
, this contradicts condition (2) above
unless
k^{\prime}\leq k
.
Using codes with
\delta_{0}\geq 1-\rho-\varepsilon
and taking
k=O(1/\varepsilon)
, we can use the above
algorithm to decode the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
arbitrarily close to list decoding capacity.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Expanders and Codes

For a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
, let
L
be the set of left vertices, and
R
be the set of right vertices. Let
A_{G}
denote the
L\times R
biadjacency matrix, and
\sigma_{2}(A_{G})
be its second largest singular value.

2.1
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander.

A
d
-regular bipartite graph
G(L,R,E)
with
|L|=|R|=n
is said to be an
(n,d,\lambda)
–expander if
\sigma_{2}(A_{G})~\leq~\lambda\cdot d
.

Infinite families of
(n,d,\lambda)
–expanders, with growing
n
as
d
and
\lambda
are constant, can be derived based on double covers of Ramanujan graphs of
[
LPS88
]
as long as
\lambda\geq\frac{2\sqrt{d-1}}{d}
.

2.2
Expander Mixing Lemma.

Given an
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander
G=(L,R,E)
and functions
f:L\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and
g:R\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the following well–known property is a simple consequence of definition of
(n,d,\lambda)
–expanders:

\Big|{\mathchoice{\underset{({\ell},{r})\sim E}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f({\ell})\cdot g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{({\ell},{r})\sim E}\left[f({\ell})\cdot g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{({\ell},{r})\sim E}\left[f({\ell})\cdot g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{({\ell},{r})\sim E}\left[f({\ell})\cdot g({r})\right]}-\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\sim L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f({\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\sim L}\left[f({\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\sim L}\left[f({\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\sim L}\left[f({\ell})\right]}\cdot\mathchoice{\underset{{r}\sim R}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\sim R}\left[g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\sim R}\left[g({r})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\sim R}\left[g({r})\right]}}\Big|~\leq~\lambda\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}\,.

When
f=\mathbb{1}_{S},g=\mathbb{1}_{T}
are indicators of sets, denote by
E(S,T)
the number of edges between
S
and
T
. Then,

\Big|{{E(S,T)}-\frac{d\cdot|S||T|}{n}}\Big|~\leq~\lambda\cdot d\cdot\sqrt{|S||T|}~\leq~\lambda\cdot d\cdot n\,.

Notation and Edge orderings.

For a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
, we index the left set by
{\ell}
and the right set by
{r}
. For a vertex
{\ell}\in L
,
we denote the set of edges incident to it by
N({\ell})
(left neighborhood), and the set of edges
incident to
{r}\in R
is denoted by
N({r})
(right neighborhood).
We use
{\ell}\sim{r}
to denote that the vertex
{\ell}\in L
is adjacent to the vertex
{r}\in R
, that is,
({\ell},{r})\in E
.

Fix an arbitrary ordering of the edges. Then there are bijections between the sets
E
,
L\times[d]
, and
R\times[d]
,
given by taking
({\ell},i)
to be the
i^{th}
edge incident on
{\ell}
, and similarly for
R\times[d]
.
Henceforth, we will implicitly assume such an ordering of the edges is fixed, and use the resulting bijections.

Codes.

Now we define the most basic notions associated with a code.

2.3
Fractional Hamming Distance.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet and let
f,g\in\Sigma^{n}
. Then the (fractional) distance
between
f,g
is defined as

{\Delta}(f,g)=\mathchoice{\underset{i\in[n]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{i}\neq g_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{i}\neq g_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{i}\neq g_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{i}\neq g_{i}\right\}}\right]}\,.

2.4
Code, distance and rate.

A code
{\mathcal{C}}
of block length
n
, distance
\delta
and rate
\rho
over the alphabet size
\Sigma
is a set
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
such that,

\rho=\frac{\log_{|\Sigma|}|{\mathcal{C}}|}{n}\;\;\text{and }\;\delta=\min_{h_{1},h_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}\colon h_{1}\neq h_{2}}{\Delta}(h_{1},h_{2}).

Such codes are succinctly represented as
[n,\rho,\delta]_{\Sigma}
.
We say
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
linear code
if
\Sigma
can be identified with a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}
is a linear subspace of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
.

2.2
Alon-Edmonds-Luby distance amplification

Alon, Bruck, Naor, Naor and Roth
[
ABN+92
]
introduced a graph-based distance amplification scheme which was generalized by Alon, Edmonds and Luby
[
AEL95
]
, and used by Guruswami and Indyk
[
GI05
]
to design linear-time unique decodable near-MDS codes.

The scheme is a three-step process involving an outer code (
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
), an inner code (
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
), and a bipartite expander
G
: (i) concatenate the outer code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
with inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
(ii) shuffle the symbols of concatenated code via edges on a
d
-regular bipartite expander graph
G
, and (iii) collect
d
-symbols on the right vertices and fold them back to produce the final code,
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
. We now formally define this procedure.

Fix an
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander
G=(L,R,E)
. Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
be an
[n,r_{\mathrm{out}},\delta_{\mathrm{out}}]_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
code and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
be a
[d,r_{\mathrm{in}},\delta_{\mathrm{in}}]_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}
code with
\lvert\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\rvert=|{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}|
, and a bijection
\varphi:\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
, realizing this.

A codeword
f
of
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
technically belongs to the space
(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{R}
but by our choice of parameters,
(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{R}
is in bijection with the set
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{E}
and one can just
fold
or
unfold
the symbols to move from one space to the other. Choosing
f
to be in
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{E}
allows us to talk about
f
viewed from left vertex set
L
or right vertex set
R
. Formally, if
f_{\ell}:=f\raisebox{-2.15277pt}{$|$}_{N(\ell)}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, we have
\varphi\raisebox{4.95134pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}(f_{\ell})\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
.

2.5
AEL Codes.

Given inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
an outer code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
, a map
\varphi
, and a graph
G
as above, the AEL code is defined as,

{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}~=~\big\{f:R\rightarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}\mid\forall\ell\in L,f_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}},\;\text{and }\big\lparen\varphi\raisebox{4.95134pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}(f_{\ell})\big\rparen_{\ell}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}\big\}.

One can also think of the code procedurally as, starting with
f^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
, and defining
f:E\rightarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
as the function such that
f_{\ell}:=\varphi(f^{*}(\ell))
. This function
f
can then be folded on the right vertices to obtain an AEL codeword.

Distance metrics for AEL Codes.

Let
f,g\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
. As we have seen, one can view these as functions on
L
, or on
R
. Associated with each of these viewpoints, we can define the following two distance functions:

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{L}(f,g)

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{\ell}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{\ell}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{\ell}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{\ell}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}

\displaystyle{\Delta}_{R}(f,g)

\displaystyle:=\mathchoice{\underset{{r}\in R}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{{r}}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\in R}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{{r}}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\in R}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{{r}}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}\in R}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{f_{{r}}\neq g_{{r}}\right\}}\right]}.

Note that for the AEL code, the distance metric is the right distance
\Delta_{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Alon, Edmonds, and Luby, proved the following result, which shows that the construction can be used to amplify the distance to
\delta_{0}
by choosing
\lambda
sufficiently small.

2.6
[
AEL95
]
.

Let
f,g\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be distinct. Then,
{\Delta}_{R}(f,g)~\geq~\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-\frac{\lambda}{{\Delta}_{L}(f,g)}\geq~\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-\frac{\lambda}{\delta_{\mathrm{out}}}
.

We wait to give a proof as we will prove a much more general version of this claim in the next section. For the rest of the paper, the alphabet of the AEL code,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
will be denoted simply as
\Sigma
.

3
Average radius generalized Singleton bound

We now prove that AEL codes satisfy the average-radius generalized Singleton bound.
We will actually prove a more general statement involving a received word where some of the coordinates are erasures, marked with a special symbol
\bot
. We will denote such partially erased received words as
\overline{g}
instead of
g
to mark the distinction.
For
\overline{g}\in(\Sigma\cup\{\bot\})^{n}
and
f\in\Sigma^{n}
, we define the distance using only the non-erasure coordinates as

\Delta(\overline{g},h)~:=~\frac{1}{n}\cdot\lvert\left\{i\in[n]~\mid~\overline{g}_{i}\in\Sigma~~\text{and}~~h_{i}\neq\overline{g}_{i}\right\}\rvert\,.

Note that if
s
denotes the fraction of erasures in
\overline{g}\in(\Sigma\cup\{\bot\})^{n}
and
h_{1},h_{2}
are two codewords from a code with distance
\delta
, then the triangle inequality implies
\Delta(\overline{g},h_{1})+\Delta(\overline{g},h_{2})\geq\delta-s
.
The following definition generalizes this to any set of (at most)
k
distinct codewords.

3.1
Average-radius list decodable with erasures.

A code
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
is
(\delta,k,\varepsilon)

average-radius
list decodable with erasures
if for any
\overline{g}\in(\Sigma\cup\{\bot\})^{n}
with (say)
s
fraction of erasures, and for any set of codewords
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
with
|{\mathcal{H}}|\leq k
, it holds that

\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Delta(\overline{g},h)~\geq~(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1)\cdot(\delta-s-\varepsilon)\,.

Note that the above definition also implies a lower bound on the distance of the code
{\mathcal{C}}
, since for
any two distinct codewords
h_{1},h_{2}
, we can take
g=h_{1}
to get
\Delta(h_{1},h_{2})\geq\delta-\varepsilon
.
Moreover, a code
{\mathcal{C}}
satisfying the above definition (even with
s=0
) must have the property that an open ball around any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
of radius
(\frac{k-1}{k})\cdot(\delta-\varepsilon)
contains at most
k-1
codewords. For
k=1/\varepsilon
, this yields a list size of
1/\varepsilon
at radius
\delta-2\varepsilon
.

We show that the stronger property above of being average-radius list decodable
with erasures

interacts nicely with the AEL construction, which yields a local-to-global result for this property
i.e., if the (constant-sized) inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
used in the AEL
construction is
(\delta,k,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, then so is the resulting (global) code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}\subseteq(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d})^{n}
.

3.2
.

Let
k\geq 1
be an integer and let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be a
code obtained using the AEL construction using
(G,{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}},{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})
, where
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
is
(\delta_{0},k_{0},\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, and
G
is a
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander for
\lambda\leq\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{out}}}{6{k_{0}}^{k_{0}}}\cdot\varepsilon
.
Then,
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
(\delta_{0},k_{0},\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures.

We need to prove that for any collection
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k}\right\}\subseteq{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
, and any
\overline{g}\in(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}\cup\{\bot\})^{R}
with fraction of erasures (say)
s
, we must have

\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-s-\varepsilon)\,.

We will prove this by induction on the size
k
of the collection
{\mathcal{H}}
.
Note that the case
k=1
is trivial since distances are non-negative.
Before proceeding to the induction step, we first need to understand the “local views" of codewords
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
from each vertex
\ell\in L
. We refer to them as local projections and develop some inequalities for them below.

Local projections and induced partitions.

To use the inequalities for the local codes
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
, we will need to consider the “local projections" of codewords
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
for each vertex
\ell\in L
, which are codewords in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
.
For a vertex
\ell\in L
, and
h\in{\mathcal{H}}
, let
h_{\ell}
be the local codeword in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
given by the values in
h
for the edges incident on
\ell
.
We know that the codewords in
{\mathcal{H}}
are pairwise distinct, however, this need not be true for their local projections
h_{1,\ell},\cdots h_{k,\ell}
.
We say that a left vertex
\ell

induces a partition

\tau_{\ell}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1,\ell},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p_{\ell},\ell})
of
{\mathcal{H}}
, wherein
h_{i},h_{j}
are in the same part if and only if
h_{i,\ell}=h_{j,\ell}
.
Since the number of partitions if bounded by
k^{k}
, many left vertices must induce the same partition. We will need the additional fact that this must be a non-trivial partition with the number of parts
p\geq 2
.

3.3
.

For any set of codewords
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{h_{1},\cdots,h_{k}\right\}
, there exists a partition
\tau^{*}

of it with at least 2 non-empty parts, and a set
L^{*}\subseteq L
such that
\tau_{\ell}=\tau^{*}

for all
\ell\in L^{*}
, and
|L^{*}|\geq\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n/{k^{k}}
.

Proof.

Let
L^{\prime}
be the set of vertices in
L
which induces a non-trivial partition, i.e., for which not all local projections of the codewords in
{\mathcal{H}}
are identical:

L^{\prime}~=~\{\ell\in L\mid\exists\,i,j\in[k],\;h_{i,\ell}\neq h_{j,\ell}\}.

Since the codewords in
{\mathcal{H}}
are distinct, we know that
|L^{\prime}|\geq\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n
.
The total number of partitions of
{\mathcal{H}}
is at most
k^{k}
, and thus among the vertices in
L^{\prime}
, there must be at least
\nicefrac{{|L^{\prime}|}}{{k^{k}}}
many vertices that induce a common partition of
{\mathcal{H}}
, which by definition of
L^{\prime}
will be non-trivial.

Throughout the proof, we will work with one such fixed partition
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
, and the corresponding set
L^{*}
. Fix an
\ell\in L^{*}
and
j\in[p]
.
By definition, the local codeword
h_{\ell}
is the same for all
h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
. We denote this common codeword by
f_{j,\ell}
and the local projection of
\overline{g}
by
\overline{g}_{\ell}\subseteq(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\{\bot\})^{d}
, where if
\overline{g}_{r}=\bot
for a vertex
r\in R
, we take projection to be
\bot
on all edges incident on
r
.
Let
s_{\ell}
denote the fraction of erasures (
\bot
symbols) in
\overline{g}_{\ell}
. We need the following inequality for local projections.

3.4
.

Let
\tau^{*}
and
L^{*}
be as above, and
\overline{g}_{\ell},s_{\ell}
and
f_{j,\ell}
be defined as above for each
\ell\in L^{*}
. Then,

\sum_{j\in[p]}\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})~\geq~(p-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-s_{\ell}-\varepsilon/2)\,.

Proof.

The claim follows from the fact that the inner code is
(\delta_{0},k_{0},\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures and
p<k\leq k_{0}
.

We will also need the following claim regarding the local erasure fractions
s_{\ell}
.

3.5
Sampling bound for erasures.

For the set
L^{*}
defined as above with
\lvert L^{*}\rvert\geq\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n/k^{k}
, and local erasure fractions
s_{\ell}
,

\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}~\leq~s+\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\,.

Proof.

Let
S=\left\{r\in R~|~\overline{g}_{r}=\bot\right\}
be the set of erasure vertices on the right with
\lvert S\rvert=s\cdot n
.
Then, applying the expander mixing lemma yields

\sum_{\ell\in L^{*}}s_{\ell}\cdot d~=~\lvert E(L^{*},S)\rvert~\leq~\frac{d}{n}\cdot\lvert L^{*}\rvert\cdot\lvert S\rvert+\lambda\cdot d\cdot n\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}~\leq~s+\lambda\cdot\frac{n}{\lvert L^{*}\rvert}\,.

Using
\lvert L^{*}\rvert\geq\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n/k^{k}
and
\lambda\leq(\delta_{\mathrm{out}}/k^{k})\cdot(\varepsilon/6)
then proves the claim.

Completing the induction step.

Recall that using the induction hypothesis, we can say that for any
\overline{g^{\prime}}
(with say
s^{\prime}
fraction of erasures) and any
{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\subseteq{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
with
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\rvert\leq k-1
, we must have
\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g^{\prime}},h)\geq(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}^{\prime}\rvert-1)\cdot(\Delta-s^{\prime}-\varepsilon)
.
Since the partition
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
is nontrivial, the cardinality
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert
of each part is at most
k-1
and we can claim by induction with the given
\overline{g}
that

\forall j\in[p]\qquad\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)\geq(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert-1)\cdot(\Delta-s-\varepsilon)\,.

The following key lemma yields a strengthening of this bound by applying the induction hypothesis with a
different
center
\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}}
for each
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
.

3.6
Inductive bound on distances.

Let the partition
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
and the set
L^{*}
be as above, and let the local projections
\overline{g}_{\ell}
and
f_{j,\ell}
be also as defined above. If the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
(\delta_{0},k-1,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, then for every
j\in[p]
,

\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)~\geq~(|{\mathcal{H}}_{j}|-1)\cdot\left(\Delta-s-\varepsilon\right)+\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\,.

Proof.

By definition of
f_{j,\ell}
, we have that for all
h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
,
h_{\ell}=f_{j,\ell}
for all
\ell\in L^{*}
. Thus, if
\overline{g}_{\ell}
and
f_{j,\ell}
differ on edge
(\ell,r)
with
\overline{g}\neq\bot
, then
r
is a
common error location
for all
h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
. We define the set

S_{j}~:=~\left\{r\in R~\mid~\overline{g}_{r}\neq\bot~\text{and}~\exists\ell\in L^{*},e=(\ell,r)~\text{such that}~(\overline{g}_{\ell})_{e}\neq(f_{j,\ell})_{e}\right\}\,.

Let
s_{j}=\lvert S_{j}\rvert/n
and let
\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}}
be obtained from
\overline{g}
by replacing symbols in
S_{j}
by
\bot
.
The total fraction of erasures in
\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}}
is
(\lvert S\rvert+\lvert S_{j}\rvert)/n=s+s_{j}
. Also,
\Delta_{R}\big\lparen\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}},h\big\rparen=\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)-s_{j}
for all
h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
, since all vertices in
S_{j}
are known to be error locations which are erased in
\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}}
. Applying the inductive hypothesis with
\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}}
now gives

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}(\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)-s_{j})~=~\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}\big\lparen\overline{g\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\lx@scalerel@obj{\lx@scalerel@obj{(j)}}}},h\big\rparen~\geq~(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert-1)\cdot(\Delta-s-s_{j}-\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow~~

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)~\geq~(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}_{j}\rvert-1)\cdot(\Delta-s-\varepsilon)+s_{j}\,.

To obtain a bound on
s_{j}
, we again use expander mixing lemma to deduce

\sum_{\ell\in L^{*}}\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\cdot d~=~\lvert E(L^{*},S_{j})\rvert~\leq~\frac{d}{n}\cdot\lvert L^{*}\rvert\cdot\lvert S_{j}\rvert+\lambda\cdot d\cdot n\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}~\leq~s_{j}+\lambda\cdot\frac{n}{\lvert L^{*}\rvert}\,.

Using
\lvert L^{*}\rvert\geq\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n/k^{k}
and
\lambda\leq(\delta_{\mathrm{out}}/k^{k})\cdot(\varepsilon/6)
gives the required bound.

We can now prove the induction step for the set
{\mathcal{H}}=\left\{h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\right\}
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

The proof, as mentioned earlier, is by induction on
k
.

Let
L^{*}
and
\tau^{*}=({\mathcal{H}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{H}}_{p})
be as above. We use the induction hypothesis to apply the bound from
Lemma

3.6
to each part
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
which has size at most
k-1
as
\tau^{*}
is non-trivial. This gives,

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)~=~\sum_{j\in[p]}\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}_{j}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)

\displaystyle~\geq~\sum_{j\in[p]}\left((|{\mathcal{H}}_{j}|-1)\cdot\left(\Delta-s-\varepsilon\right)+\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\right)

\displaystyle~=~(k-p)\cdot\left(\Delta-s-\varepsilon\right)+\sum_{j\in[p]}\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}-\frac{p\varepsilon}{6}.

Using local distance inequality from
3.4
and the sampling bound from
3.5
, we can bound the second term as

\sum_{j\in[p]}\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})\right]}~\geq~(p-1)\cdot\Big\lparen\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L^{*}}\left[s_{\ell}\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\rparen~\geq~(p-1)\cdot\Big\lparen\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-s-\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}\Big\rparen\,.

Combining the above bounds and using
\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\geq\Delta
gives,

\displaystyle\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Delta_{R}(\overline{g},h)

\displaystyle~\geq~(k-p)\cdot(\Delta-s-\varepsilon)+(p-1)\cdot\Big\lparen\Delta-s-\frac{2\varepsilon}{3}\Big\rparen-\frac{p\varepsilon}{6}

\displaystyle~=~(k-1)\cdot(\Delta-s-\varepsilon)+\frac{(p-1)\varepsilon}{3}-\frac{p\varepsilon}{6}\,,

which completes the proof since
p\geq 2
.

As we will prove in
Section

4
, it is easy to observe using known results by Alrabiah, Guruswami and Li
[
AGL24b
]
that a random
linear code satisfies
Definition

3.1
with high probability, and can thus be used as the
inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
.
Since the inner code is a constant-sized object, we can search over all linear codes in
({\mathbb{F}}_{q})^{d}

of dimension
\rho\cdot d
for a given rate
\rho
, and the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
then yields an explicit
construction of codes achieving the generalized Singleton bound.
Moreover, if the inner code is required to be fully explicit, it can also be obtained from folded Reed-Solomon codes, using the results by Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
.

3.7
.

For every
\rho,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist explicit inner codes
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
and an infinite family of explicit codes
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}\subseteq({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d})^{n}
obtained via the AEL construction that satisfy:

1.

\rho({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})\geq\rho
.

2.

For any
g\in({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d})^{n}
and any
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
with
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert\leq k
that

\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}\Delta(g,h)~\geq~(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1)\cdot(1-\rho-\varepsilon)\,.

3.

The alphabet size
q^{d}
of the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
can be taken to be
2^{O(k^{3k}/\varepsilon^{9})}
.

4.

{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is characterized by parity checks of size
O(k^{2k}/\varepsilon^{11})
over the field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

Proof.

Let
d=O(k^{2k}/\varepsilon^{8})
be such that there exist explicit families of
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander graphs (for arbitrarily large
n
) with
\lambda\leq\varepsilon^{4}/(2^{18}k^{k})
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}
be a code given by
Corollary

4.3
, with rate
\rho_{\mathrm{in}}=\rho+\varepsilon/4
, which is
(1-\rho,k,\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures. Note that the alphabet size
q
for
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
can be taken to be
2^{O(k+1/\varepsilon)}
.
Finally, let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}\subseteq({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\rho_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot d})^{n}
be an outer (linear) code with rate
\rho_{\mathrm{out}}=1-\varepsilon/4
and distance (say)
\delta_{\mathrm{out}}=\varepsilon^{3}/2^{15}
. Explicit families of such codes can be also be obtained (for example) via expander-based Tanner code constructions (see Theorem 11.4.6 and Corollary 11.4.8 in
[
GRS23
]
). Using Tanner codes also gives that
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
has parity checks of size at most
O(1/\varepsilon^{3})
.

Given the above parameters, we have

\rho({{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}})~\geq~\rho_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot\rho_{\mathrm{in}}~=~(1-\varepsilon/4)\cdot(\rho+\varepsilon/4)~\geq~\rho
.
Since
\lambda\leq\varepsilon\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{out}}/(6k^{k})
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
is
(1-\rho,k,\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, we can use
Theorem

3.2
to conclude that
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
(1-\rho,k,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable (with erasures) which yields the second condition.
Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
has parity checks of
O(1/\varepsilon^{3})
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}
, and each symbol of
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is a function of at most
d
symbols from
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
(encoded via
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
),
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
can be taken to have parity checks of size at most
O(d\cdot(1/\varepsilon^{3}))=O(k^{2k}/\varepsilon^{11})
.
Finally, we note that the alphabet size of the code
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
is
q^{d}=\exp\left(O((k+1/\varepsilon)\cdot(k^{2k}/\varepsilon^{8}))\right)=\exp\left(k^{3k}/\varepsilon^{9}\right)
, which proves the claim.

A weaker consequence of
Definition

3.1
.

We also state the following consequence of
Definition

3.1
, which still yields a
strengthening of the average distance inequality and the generalized Singleton bound (when
instantiated with the appropriate code), and may be of independent interest.
Note that this statement also yields a corollary of
Theorem

3.2
which is simply in
terms of center
g\in\Sigma^{n}
with no erasure symbols, and yields an advantage over the distance
inequality, in terms of the error locations which are
common
to all the codewords
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}
.

3.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
be
(\delta_{0},k_{0},\varepsilon)
average radius list-decodable with
erasures. Then, for any
g\in\Sigma^{n}
, any
k\leq k_{0}
and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we have
that

\sum_{i\in[k]}\Delta(g,h_{i})~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{\underset{r\in[n]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}\,.

Proof.

Define
\overline{g}\in\Sigma^{n}
as

\overline{g}_{r}~=~\begin{cases}\bot&\text{if}~g_{r}\neq h_{i,r}~\forall i\in[k]\\
g_{r}&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Note that the fraction of erasure symbols is
s=\mathchoice{\underset{r\in[n]}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\in[n]}\left[\prod_{i\in[k]}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{h_{i,r}\neq g_{r}\right\}}\right]}
and
\Delta(\overline{g},h_{i})=\Delta(g,h)-s
for all
i\in[k]
. Applying

Definition

3.1
with
\overline{g}
gives

\sum_{i\in[k]}(\Delta(g,h_{i})-s)~=~\sum_{i\in[k]}\Delta(\overline{g},h_{i})~\geq~(k-1)\cdot(\delta_{0}-s-\varepsilon)\,,

and rearranging proves the claim.

3.9
.

A reader might notice that the definition of the
\overline{g}
is the same as used in the proof of

Lemma

3.6
.
In fact, it is easy to see that the consequence
Lemma

3.8
can directly be
proved via induction using the same proof as
Theorem

3.2
, which avoids using a

\overline{g}
with erasures as part of the induction (although one still needs the list decodability
with erasures for the inner code
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
).
While we chose to prove the stronger local-to-global statement as
Theorem

3.2

above, for the algorithmic application we will only prove an algorithmic analogue of

Lemma

3.8
to avoid technical issues with keeping track of arbitrary erasure patterns.

4
Inner Codes meeting generalized Singleton Bound

In this section, we will look at two constructions of inner codes that are average-radius list decodable with erasures, the property we need to instantiate our construction. These will be a random linear code, and folded Reed-Solomon codes.

In the literature, the property of being average-radius list decodable is usually defined without reference to any erasures (i.e.,
s=0
in
Definition

3.1
). Formally, we say that
{\mathcal{C}}\subseteq\Sigma^{n}
is
(\delta,k,\varepsilon)

average-radius list decodable
if for all
g\in\Sigma^{n}
and all
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}
with
\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert\leq k
, we have

\sum_{h\in{\mathcal{H}}}{\Delta(g,h)}~\geq~\left(\lvert{\mathcal{H}}\rvert-1\right)\cdot(\delta-\varepsilon)\,.

The other way of looking at erasures is via puncturings, and we will now see that if a code and its puncturings are average-radius list decodable in the above sense, then it is average-radius list decodable with erasures.

Erasures and Puncturings.

Let
C\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
be a code,
S\subseteq[n]
, and denote
s:=\nicefrac{{|S|}}{{n}}
. Let
\rho=\rho(C)
denote the rate of
C
. Define
C_{S}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{(1-s)n}
as the punctured code obtained by removing the coordinates in
S
.

4.1
.

If for each
S\subseteq[n]
with
s\leq 1-\rho
,
C_{S}
is
(1-\rho,L,\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s})
average-radius list decodable, then
C
is
(1-\rho,L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures.

Proof.

Let
\overline{g}
have erasures in a set
S\subseteq[n]
, and denote by
g_{S}
, the punctured vectored with these erasure locations removed. Similarly for any list of
L
codewords
{\mathcal{H}}\subseteq C
, denote the punctured list by
{\mathcal{H}}_{S}
. If the code
C_{S}
is
(L,\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s})
average-radius list decodable (without erasures), then,

\sum_{h_{S}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{S}}\Delta(g_{S},h_{S})~\geq~(L-1)\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\rho(C_{S})-\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s}\Big\rparen\,.

Observe that if
C
is a rate
\rho
code, then
\rho(C_{S})\leq\frac{\rho}{1-s}
(in fact,

\rho(C_{S})=\rho
