if the distance of
C_{S}
is greater than 0, but we only need the one-sided inequality). Also
\Delta(\overline{g},h)\geq\Delta(g_{S},h_{S})\cdot(1-s)
. Multiplying the entire equation by
(1-s)
and plugging this in, we get,

\sum_{h_{S}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{S}}\Delta(\overline{g},h)~\geq~(L-1)\cdot\Big\lparen 1-s-\rho-\varepsilon\Big\rparen\,.\qquad\hbox{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}

Random Linear Codes.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n,\rho n,q}
be the uniform distribution over
n\times\rho n
-matrices over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, i.e., where each entry of the matrix is picked uniformly at random from
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Let
C=\mathrm{im}(G)
where
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{n,\rho n,q}
. Then, for any fixed
S\subseteq[n]
,
C_{S}=\mathrm{im}(G_{S})
where
G_{S}\sim\mathcal{G}_{n-|S|,\rho n,q}
.

4.2
[
AGL24b
, Thm. 1.3]
.

Fix an integer
L\geq 1
,
q\geq 2\cdot 2^{10L/\varepsilon}
, and
\rho,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Then for sufficiently large
n
, a random linear code
C=\mathrm{im}(G)
where
G\sim\mathcal{G}_{n,\,\rho n,q}
, is
(1-\rho,L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with probablity at least
1-\kappa
, where,

\kappa~=~\Big\lparen\frac{c_{L,\varepsilon}}{q}\Big\rparen^{\lfloor\frac{\varepsilon n}{2}\rfloor},\;\text{ for }c_{L,\varepsilon}<2\cdot 2^{10L/\varepsilon}.

Proof.

Their definition of average-radius list decodable gives an inequality for exactly
\ell
distinct codewords. Thus, we obtain this bound by taking a union over their bounds for
\ell=1,\cdots,L
.

4.3
.

Let
C
be a random linear code as generated above for
q\geq 2^{\nicefrac{{(10L+2)}}{{\varepsilon}}}
. Then,
with probability at least
(1-2^{-n/3})
,
C
is
(1-\rho,L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures.

Proof.

We will show that with high probability, for all
S\subseteq[n]
of size at most
|S|\leq(1-\rho)\cdot n
, the punctured code
C_{S}
is
(1-\rho(C_{S}),L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable.

For a fixed
S
of fractional size
s\leq 1-\rho
, we have that
C_{S}
is not

(1-\rho(C_{S}),L,\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s})
average-radius list-decodable with probablity at most,

\leq\Big\lparen\frac{c_{L,\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s}}}{q}\Big\rparen^{\Big\lfloor\frac{\frac{\varepsilon}{1-s}(1-s)n}{2}\Big\rfloor}\leq\Big\lparen\frac{c_{L,\varepsilon}}{q}\Big\rparen^{\lfloor\frac{\varepsilon n}{2}\rfloor}=\kappa.

There are at most
2^{n}
choices of the subsets
S
, and thus by a union bound, the probability that
all the punctured codes are average-radius list-decodable is at least

1-2^{n}\kappa~\geq~1-2^{n}\cdot 2^{(10L/\varepsilon+1)\cdot\varepsilon n/2}\cdot q^{-\lfloor\varepsilon n/2\rfloor}~\geq~1-2^{-n/3}\,,

for
q\geq 2^{2/\varepsilon}\cdot 2^{10L/\varepsilon}
and
n\geq 60L+12
.

Thus, for a large enough alphabet size, random linear codes are average-radius list-decodable with erasures.

Folded Reed–Solomon Codes.

A recent work of Chen and Zhang
[
CZ24
]
shows that explicit folded Reed–Solomon (RS) codes are also average-radius list-decodable. Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}[x]
denote the set of polynomials with
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-coefficients, and
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
be the multiplicative cyclic group of non-zero elements.

4.4
Folded RS Codes.

Fix
n,b>0
,
\rho\in(0,1)
, and
q\geq bn
. Let
\gamma
be a generator of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{*}
, and pick
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
. For
f\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}[x]
, let
\Gamma_{i}=(f(\alpha_{i}),f(\gamma\alpha_{i}),\cdots,f(\gamma^{b-1}\alpha_{i}))

Then,

\mathrm{FRS}^{b,\gamma}_{n,\rho}(\vec{\alpha})=\{(\Gamma_{1},\cdots,\Gamma_{n})\mid\deg(f)<\rho bn\}\subseteq({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b})^{n}.

The code is called appropriate if
\{\gamma^{i}\alpha_{j}\mid 0\leq i\leq b-1,j\in[n]\}
has size
bn
, i.e., all values are distinct.

4.5
[
CZ24
, Thm. 1.3]
.

For any integer
L\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
b\geq L/\varepsilon
. Then, an appropriate folded Reed-Solomon code,
\mathrm{FRS}^{b,\gamma}_{n,\rho}(\vec{\alpha})
, is
(1-\rho,L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable where
\rho
is the rate of this folded code.

Note that puncturing the folded Reed–Solomon code is equivalent to the FRS code over a puncturing of
\vec{\alpha}
. Clearly, a puncturing of an appropriate folded Reed–Solomon code is also an appropriate Reed–Solomon code, and thus using
4.1
, one obtains:

4.6
.

For any integer
L\geq 1
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, set
b=L/\varepsilon
. Then, an appropriate folded Reed-Solomon code,
\mathrm{FRS}^{s,\gamma}_{n,k}(\vec{\alpha})
, is
(1-\rho,L,\varepsilon)
average-radius list decodable with erasures.

5
Decoding using SoS up to the generalized Singleton bound

Until now, we have proved that when the inner and outer codes as well as the graph used in AEL amplification are suitably chosen, then the list of codewords around an arbitrary center (corrupted codeword) is of small size.
In this section, we describe a polynomial time algorithm that takes as input the corrupted codeword, and outputs this list.

This algorithm is based on the Sum-of-Squares (SoS) hierarchy of semidefinite programs, which gives a systematic way of tightening convex relaxations. SoS has been used before for decoding algorithms for codes constructed using spectral expanders in
[
AJQ+20
,
JQST20
,
RR23
,
JST23
]
.
Among these,
[
JST23
]
used the SoS hierarchy to give a list decoding algorithm for AEL up to the Johnson bound, yielding rate
R
codes efficiently decodable up to
1-\sqrt{R}-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon>0
.
We will heavily rely on their framework but will improve the decoding radius to
1-R-\varepsilon
by proving an SoS analog of the generalized Singleton bound for appropriately instantiated AEL amplification.

Before going into the proof, we describe additional preliminaries for the SoS hierarchy. Readers familiar with the general terms and concepts can skip ahead to
Section

5.2
where we define a specific SoS relaxation for the AEL code, and prove an SoS analog of the generalized Singleton bound. Finally,
Section

5.3
describes the decoding algorithm in detail.

5.1
Additional Preliminaries: Sum-of-Squares Hierarchy

The sum-of-squares hierarchy of semidefinite programs (SDPs) provides a family of increasingly
powerful convex relaxations for several optimization problems.
Each “level"
t
of the hierarchy is parametrized by a set of constraints corresponding to
polynomials of degree at most
t
in the optimization variables. While the relaxations in the
hierarchy can be viewed as semidefinite programs of size
n^{O(t)}

[
BS14
,
FKP19
]
,
it is often convenient to view the solution as a linear operator, called the “pseudoexpectation" operator.

Pseudoexpectations

Let
t
be a positive even integer and fix an alphabet
\Sigma
of size
s
. Let
{\mathbf{Z}}=\{Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in\Sigma}
be a collection of variables and
{\mathbb{R}}[{\mathbf{Z}}]^{\leq t}
be the vector space of polynomials of degree at most
t
in the variables
{\mathbf{Z}}
(including the constants).

5.1
Constrained Pseudoexpectations.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=\left\{f_{1}=0,\ldots,f_{m}=0,g_{1}\geq 0,\ldots,g_{r}\geq 0\right\}
be a system of
polynomial constraints in
{\mathbf{Z}}
, with each polynomial in
{\mathcal{S}}
of degree at most
t
. We say
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
is a pseudoexpectation operator of SoS-degree
t
, over the variables
{\mathbf{Z}}
respecting
{\mathcal{S}}
, if it is a linear operator
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}:{\mathbb{R}}[{\mathbf{Z}}]^{\leq t}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that:

1.

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1\right]}=1
.

2.

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\right]}\geq 0
if
p
is a polynomial in
{\mathbf{Z}}=\{Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in\Sigma}
of degree
\leq t/2
.

3.

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p\cdot f_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p\cdot f_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p\cdot f_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p\cdot f_{i}\right]}=0
,
\forall\,i\in[m]
and
\forall\,p
such that
\deg(p\cdot f_{i})\leq t
.

4.

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}\right]}\geq 0
,
\forall\,S\subseteq[r]
and
\forall\,p
such that
\deg(p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i})\leq t
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over the set of assignments,
\Sigma^{m}
. Define the following collection of random variables,

{\mathbf{Z}}=\big\{\,Z_{i,j}={{\mathbf{1}}\left\{i\mapsto j\right\}}\,\mid\,i\in[m],\,j\in\Sigma\big\}.

Then, setting
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}=\mathchoice{\underset{\mu}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}
for any polynomial
p(\cdot)
defines an (unconstrained) pseudoexpectation operator. However, the reverse is not true when
t<m
, and there can be degree-
t
pseudoexpectations that do not correspond to any genuine distribution,
\mu
. Therefore, the set of all pseudoexpectations should be seen as a relaxation for the set of all possible distributions over such assignments. The upshot of this relaxation is that it is possible to optimize over the set. Under certain conditions on the bit-complexity of solutions
[
O’D17
,
RW17
]
, one can optimize over the set of degree-
t
pseudoexpectations in time
m^{O(t)}
via SDPs.

Local constraints and local functions.

Any constraint that involves at most
k
variables from
{\mathbf{Z}}
, with
k\leq t
, can be written as a degree-
k
polynomial, and such constraints may be enforced into the SoS solution.
In particular, we will always consider the following canonical constraints on the variables
{\mathbf{Z}}
.

Z_{i,j}^{2}=Z_{i,j},\ \forall\,i\in[m],j\in\Sigma\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j}Z_{i,j}=1,\ \forall\,i\in[m]\,.

We will also consider additional constraints and corresponding polynomials, defined by “local" functions. For any
f\in\Sigma^{m}
and
M\subseteq[m]
, we use
f_{M}
to denote the restriction
f|_{M}
, and
f_{i}
to denote
f_{\{i\}}
for convenience.

5.2
k
-local function.

A function
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is called
k
-local if there is a set
M\subseteq[m]
of size
k
such that
\mu(f)
only depends on
\left\{f(i)\right\}_{i\in M}
, or equivalently,
\mu(f)
only depends on
f|_{M}
.

If
\mu
is
k
-local, we abuse notation to also use
\mu:\Sigma^{M}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mu(\alpha)=\mu(f)
for any
f
such that
f|_{M}=\alpha
. It will be clear from the input to the function
\mu
whether we are using
\mu
as a function on
\Sigma^{m}
or
\Sigma^{M}
.

Let
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a
k
-local function that depends on coordinates
M\subseteq[m]
with
|M|=k
. Then
\mu
can be written as a degree-
k
polynomial
P_{\mu}
in
{\mathbf{Z}}
:

P_{\mu}({\mathbf{Z}})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}}\Big\lparen\mu(\alpha)\cdot\prod_{i\in M}Z_{i,\alpha_{i}}\Big\rparen

With some abuse of notation, we let
\mu({\mathbf{Z}})
denote
P_{\mu}({\mathbf{Z}})
. We will use such
k
-local
functions inside
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
freely without worrying about their polynomial
representation. For example,
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\neq j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\neq j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\neq j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}\neq j\right\}}\right]}
denotes
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1-Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1-Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1-Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1-Z_{i,j}\right]}
. Likewise, sometimes we will say we set
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j
to mean that we set
Z_{i,j}=1
and
Z_{i,j^{\prime}}=0
for all
j^{\prime}\in\Sigma\backslash\{j\}
.

The notion of
k
-local functions can also be extended from real-valued functions to vector-valued functions straightforwardly.

5.3
Vector-valued local functions.

A function
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is
k
-local if the
N
real valued functions corresponding to the
N
coordinates are all
k
-local. Note that these different coordinate functions may depend on different sets of variables, as long as the number is at most
k
for each of the functions.

Local distribution view of SoS

It will be convenient to use a shorthand for the function
{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}
, and we will use
{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}
. Likewise, we use
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,j}
as a shorthand for the function
{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j\right\}}
. That is, henceforth,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}~=~\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}\right]}~=~\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\prod_{a\in A}Z_{a,\alpha_{a}}\Big]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,j}\right]}~=~\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{i}=j\right\}}\right]}=\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[Z_{i,j}\right]}

Note that for any
A\subseteq[m]
with
\left\lvert A\right\rvert=k\leq t/2
,

\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{k}}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{a\in A}\bigg\lparen\sum_{j\in\Sigma}Z_{a,j}\bigg\rparen\bigg]=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{a\in A}Z_{a,\alpha_{a}}\bigg]=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\prod_{a\in A}Z^{2}_{a,\alpha_{a}}\bigg]\geq 0\,.

Thus, the values
\left\{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{A,\alpha}\right]}\right\}_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{A}}
define a distribution
over
\Sigma^{k}
. We call this the local distribution for
{\mathbf{Z}}_{A}
, or simply for
A
.
Let
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a
k
-local function for
k\leq t/2
, depending on
M\subseteq[m]
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}~=~\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}}\bigg\lparen\mu(\alpha)\cdot\prod_{i\in M}Z_{i,\alpha_{i}}\bigg\rparen\bigg]~=~\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}}\mu(\alpha)\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\prod_{i\in M}Z_{i,\alpha_{i}}\Big]~=~\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}}\mu(\alpha)\cdot\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{M,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{M,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{M,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{M,\alpha}\right]}

That is,
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}
can be seen as the expected value of the function
\mu
under the local distribution for
M
.

5.4
.

Let
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
be a degree-
t
pseudoexpectation. For
k\leq t/2
, let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}

be two
k
-local functions on
\Sigma^{m}
, depending on the same set of coordinates
M
, and

\mu_{1}(\alpha)\leq\mu_{2}(\alpha)~~\forall\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}
. Then
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\leq\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{D}}_{M}
be the local distribution induced by
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
for
{\mathbf{Z}}_{M}
. Then

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}=\mathchoice{\underset{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu_{1}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{1}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{1}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{1}(\alpha)\right]}
, and
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}=\mathchoice{\underset{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu_{2}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{2}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{2}(\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\alpha\sim{\mathcal{D}}_{M}}\left[\mu_{2}(\alpha)\right]}
, which implies
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{1}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\leq\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mu_{2}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}
.

The previous claim allows us to replace any local function inside
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
by another local function that dominates it. We will make extensive use of this fact.

Covariance for SoS solutions

Given two sets
S,T\subseteq[m]
with
|S|,|T|\leq k/4
, we can define the covariance between indicator random variables of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}_{T}
taking values
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively, according to the local distribution over
S\cup T
. This is formalized in the next definition.

5.5
.

Let
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
be a pseudodistribution operator of SoS-degree-
t
, and
S,T
are two sets of
size at most
t/4
, and
\alpha\in\Sigma^{S}
,
\beta\in\Sigma^{T}
, we define the pseudo-covariance and
pseudo-variance,

\displaystyle\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta})~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\cdot{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\cdot{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\cdot{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\cdot{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}-\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}\,\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}\right]}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}~

\displaystyle=~\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha})

The above definition is extended to pseudo-covariance and pseudo-variance for pairs of sets
S,T
,
as the sum of absolute value of pseudo-covariance for all pairs
\alpha,\beta
:

\displaystyle\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T})~

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{S},\beta\in\Sigma^{T}}\left\lvert\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}~

\displaystyle=~\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{S}}\left\lvert\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}\right\rvert

We will need the fact that
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}
is bounded above by 1, since,

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}\right]}~=~\sum_{\alpha}\left\lvert\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}\right\rvert~=~\sum_{\alpha}\left(\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}-\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}^{2}\right)~\leq~\sum_{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}^{2}\right]}~=~\sum_{\alpha}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}\right]}~=~1.

Conditioning SoS solutions.

We will also make use of conditioned pseudoexpectation operators, which may be defined in a way
similar to usual conditioning for true expectation operators, as long as the event we condition on
is local.
The conditioned SoS solution is of a smaller degree but continues to respect the constraints that the original solution respects.

5.6
Conditioned SoS Solution.

Let
F\subseteq\Sigma^{m}
be subset (to be thought of as an event) such that
{\mathbf{1}}_{F}:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is a
k
-local function. Then for every
t>2k
, we can condition a pseudoexpectation operator of SoS-degree
t
on
F
to obtain a new conditioned pseudoexpectation operator
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\;|\;F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\;|\;F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\;|\;F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\;|\;F\right]}
of SoS-degree
t-2k
, as long as
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}>0
. The conditioned SoS solution is given by

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\;|\;F({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\;|\;F({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\;|\;F({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\;|\;F({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}:=\frac{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}}{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}^{2}_{F}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}}

where
p
is any polynomial of degree at most
t-2k
.

We can also define pseudocovariances and pseudo-variances for the conditioned SoS solutions.

5.7
Pseudocovariance.

Let
F\subseteq\Sigma^{m}
be an event such that
{\mathbf{1}}_{F}
is
k
-local, and let
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
be a pseudoexpectation operator of degree
t
, with
t>2k
. Let
S,T
be two sets of size at most
\frac{t-2k}{2}
each. Then the pseudocovariance between
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}
for the solution conditioned on event
F
is defined as,

\displaystyle\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F)~=~\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}-\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|F\right]}~\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|F\right]}

We also define the pseudocovariance between
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}
and
{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}
after
conditioning on a random assignment for some
{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}
with
V\subseteq[m]
.
Note that here the random assignment for
{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}
is chosen according to the local distribution for
the set
V
.

5.8
Pseudocovariance for conditioned pseudoexpectation operators.

\displaystyle\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V})~=~\sum_{\gamma\in\Sigma^{V}}\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T,\beta}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}=\gamma)\cdot\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{V,\gamma}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{V,\gamma}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{V,\gamma}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{V,\gamma}\right]}

And we likewise define
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,\alpha}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}
,
\tildecov({\mathbf{Z}}_{S},{\mathbf{Z}}_{T}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V})
and
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{S}|{\mathbf{Z}}_{V}\right]}
.

5.2
Pseudocodewords achieve the generalized Singleton bound

We will show that the proof of list decodability established for integral codewords can be extended to SoS relaxations of these codewords, under certain average-case low covariance conditions.
First, we describe what an SoS relaxation of an AEL codeword looks like. This relaxation will be such that true codewords are always feasible solutions, but there may be other feasible solutions. These feasible solutions are therefore called
pseudocodewords
.

The main goal will be to show that for some large constant
t
depending on
k
and
\varepsilon
, but independent of
n
, the degree-
t
SoS relaxation is tight enough to decode up to radius
\frac{k-1}{k}(1-R-\varepsilon)
. We will actually work with
k
-tuples of pseudocodewords, and the show that this tuple—after some random conditioning—satisfies a generalized Singleton bound just like a list of true (distinct) codewords.

SoS relaxations for AEL codewords

Let
G(L,R,E)
be the bipartite
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander on which the AEL code is defined, and let
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
be the inner code.

Before going into the details of the SoS relaxation for AEL codes, let us set up some convenient notation. For sets
S\subseteq[k]
and
F\subseteq E
, we use
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,F}
to index their Cartesian product
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S\times F}
. Further, since we will be often dealing with the case when
F=N({\ell})
or
F=N({r})
, we will use
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,{\ell}}
as a shorthand for
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,N({\ell})}
, and similarly use
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,{r}}
as a shorthand for
{\mathbf{Z}}_{S,N({r})}
. For example,

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k],{\ell}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k],{\ell}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k],{\ell}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k],{\ell}}\right]}=\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathrm{Var}}}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})}\right]}.

5.9
k
-tuple of pseudocodewords.

A
k
-tuple of psueocodewords of degree-
t
is a pseudoexpectation operator
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
of SoS-degree-
t
defined on the variables
\{Z_{i,e}\}_{i\in[k],e\in E}
over the alphabet
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
that respects the following constraints:

\forall i\in[k],\forall{\ell}\in L,~~~{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,N({\ell})}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}

It is easy to see that any
k
strings
f_{1},\cdots,f_{k}
in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}^{L}
can be used to define a
k
-tuple of pseudocodeword, by simply setting
{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,e}
to be
(f_{i})_{e}
for all
i\in[k],e\in E
. Note that these strings need not be distinct, as written. However, the set of
k
-tuples of pseudocodewords is more general that just integral strings, and in particular, is convex. However, we can still generalize notions like distance for these objects.

5.10
Distances and pseudocodewords.

For an
i\in[k]
, we define the left and right distances between the
i^{th}
component of a
k
-tuple of pseudocodeword
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
of SoS-degree
t\geq 2d
and any string
g\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{E}
as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}

\eta
-goodness

Instead of working with arbitrary pseudocodewords, we will work with those that have small pseudocovariances across a typical edge in
G
. This key property, called
\eta
-goodness, has been used in prior works and we extend its definition from
[
JST23
]
to
k
-tuple of pseudocodewords as follows:

5.12
\eta
-good pseudocodeword.

A
k
-tuple of pseudocodewords
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
of degree at least
2kd
is called
\eta
-good if

\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell},{r}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell},{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell},{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell},{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}{{\tildecov}\left[{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({\ell})},{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]\times N({r})}\right]}\right]}\leq\eta.

The key upshot of having this property is
Lemma

A.1
which we use in our proof. Moreover, one can obtain such
\eta
-good pseudocodewords by randomly conditioning an SoS solution. We relegate the proof of these details to
Appendix

A
as they are an adaptation of earlier proofs.

The main result of this section is the following generalization of
Lemma

3.8
to
\eta
-good pseudocodewords.

5.13
.

Let
k_{0}\geq 1
be an integer and let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be a
code obtained using the AEL construction using

-expander.
Let
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
be an
\eta
-good
k_{0}
-tuple of pseudocodewords that satisfies the following pairwise distance property on the left:

\forall i,i^{\prime}\in[k_{0}]\text{ with }i\neq i^{\prime},\qquad\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}\geq\beta\,.

Further, assume that
\lambda\leq\frac{\beta}{12k_{0}^{k_{0}}}\cdot\varepsilon
and
\eta\leq\frac{\beta}{12k_{0}^{k_{0}}}\cdot\varepsilon
. Then, for any

,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[k_{0}]}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}~

\displaystyle\geq~(k_{0}-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{\underset{{r}\in \displaystyle=~(k_{0}-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}.

Just like in the proof of our main theorem in
Section

3
, we need preparatory claims about the existence of a nice partition and (a variant of)
L^{*}
. We start by proving analytic generalizations of
3.3
and
Lemma

3.6
. Let
{\mathrm{T}}
denote the set of all partitions
1
1

1

Note that now we work with partitions of
[k]
rather a list of codewords
{\mathcal{H}}
.
of
[k]
. For an
{\ell}\in L
and
\tau\in{\mathrm{T}}
, define the local function,

\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau}({\mathbf{Z}}):={{\mathbf{1}}\left\{({\mathbf{Z}}_{1,{\ell}},{\mathbf{Z}}_{2,{\ell}},\cdots,{\mathbf{Z}}_{k,{\ell}})\text{ induces partition }\tau\right\}}.

By definition, for every
{\ell}\in L
,

\sum_{\tau\in{\mathrm{T}}}\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau}({\mathbf{Z}})=1.

Let
\tau_{1}\in{\mathrm{T}}
be the trivial partition with only 1 part. In the integral proof, we said that there must be a non-trivial partition that is induced on a significant fraction of left vertices, and used it to define the set
L^{*}
. For tuples of pseudocodewords, each left vertex will be inducing a distribution over all possible partitions, and we will argue that there is a partition
\tau^{*}
that receives a significant mass among these distributions on average over all
{\ell}\in L
. The indicator of this partition
\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})
will then play the role of the indicator of the set
L^{*}
in integral proof.

5.14
Generalization of
3.3
.

There exists a
\tau^{*}\in{\mathrm{T}}\setminus\{\tau_{1}\}
such that

\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\in L}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}\geq\frac{\beta}{k^{k}}.

Proof.

If
({\mathbf{Z}}_{1,{\ell}},{\mathbf{Z}}_{2,{\ell}},\cdots,{\mathbf{Z}}_{k,{\ell}})
induces the partition
\tau_{1}
, then
{\mathbf{Z}}_{1,{\ell}}={\mathbf{Z}}_{2,{\ell}}
. That is,

\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau_{1}}({\mathbf{Z}})\leq{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{1,{\ell}}={\mathbf{Z}}_{2,{\ell}}\right\}}.

Capturing common error locations

Suppose the partition
\tau^{*}
is given by
[k]=(K_{1},\cdots,K_{p})
for some
1<p<k
. Henceforth, we will be working with this fixed partition. We define a function,
{\mathsf{D}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})
, which is an indicator of whether
{r}\in R
is a common error location for the pseudocodewords indexed by
S\subseteq[k]
.

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle=\Pi_{i\in S}{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{g_{{r}}\neq{\mathbf{Z}}_{i,{r}}\right\}}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}}_{S,e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle={\mathsf{D}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}),\text{ where }e=({\ell},{r}).

Of course, if a vertex
{r}
is a common error location for all
k
pseudocodewords, then it is also a common error location for a subset, implying

{\mathsf{D}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})~\geq~{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}).

We will also need to track the error locations common to
S
but not to
[k]
, so we define two additional shorthands:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})~

\displaystyle=~{\mathsf{D}}_{S,{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})~-~{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})

\displaystyle{\mathsf{F}}_{S,e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})~

\displaystyle=~{\mathsf{F}}_{S,r}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}),\text{ where }e=({\ell},{r}).

Lemma

3.6
,
3.4
, and
3.5
.

L^{*}
can be carried out by reweighing according to the indicator function
\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}
for the
\tau^{*}
partition.

Proof.

), and the second utilizes the
\eta

Lemma

),

The second lemma showed that the number of common error locations increases when only considering a subset of indices
K_{j}\subseteq[k]
, and this increase can be lower bounded in terms of the distance between
g_{{\ell}}
(actually,
\overline{g}_{\ell}
) and the common local projections of
K_{j}
, averaged over
L^{*}
. The second term on the RHS in the lemma below is the analog of these errors between
\overline{g}_{\ell}
and common local projections
f_{j}
, averaged over
L^{*}
.

5.16
Generalization of
Lemma

3.6
.

For any part
K_{j}
in the partition
\tau^{*}
, we have,

Proof.

By using the definition of the functions
{\mathsf{D}},{\mathsf{F}}
and some rearragement, we get,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{{r}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{{r}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})+(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}))\cdot \displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{{r}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}+\mathchoice{\underset{{r}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{r}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mat The proof finishes by replacing the second term by the bound from
Lemma

5.15
.

Finally, we state the local inequality needed from the inner code. Since this inequality is only valid for vertices in
L^{*}
, we multiply the required equation by indicator
\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})
so that it is trivial when the indicator is 0. Subject to this indicator being 1, the inequality says that the generalized Singleton bound with erasures holds for the inner code. Note that this bound holds for each vertex in
L^{*}
unlike previous sampling lemmas which only work in an average sense over
L^{*}
.

5.17
Generalization of
3.4
.

For every
{\ell}\in L
and for every
j\in[p]
,

\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\bigg\lparen\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\bigg\rparen~\geq~\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot(p-1)\Big\lparen\delta_{0}-\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\rparen.

Proof.

Let
M_{{\ell}}\subseteq E
be the union of edge neighborhoods over vertices in
R
that are adjacent to
{\ell}
. That is,

M_{{\ell}}=\bigcup_{{r}\sim{\ell}}N({r})

Let us consider the following two local functions that depend on
[k]\times M_{{\ell}}
.

\displaystyle\mu_{1}(\cdot)~

\displaystyle=~\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}(\cdot)\;\bigg\lparen\;\sum_{j\in[p]}\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\cdot)\right]}\,\bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(\cdot)~

\displaystyle=~\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}(\cdot)(p-1)\left(\delta_{0}-\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\cdot)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\cdot)\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)

We will prove this inequality pointwise by showing that for any
\alpha\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{[k]\times M_{{\ell}}}
,
\mu_{1}(\alpha)\geq\mu_{2}(\alpha)
.

If
\alpha
is such that
\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}(\alpha)=0
, then the inequality is trivially true. Henceforth, we assume that
\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}(\alpha)=1
. This means that
(\alpha_{1,{\ell}},\alpha_{2,{\ell}},\cdots,\alpha_{k,{\ell}})
induces the partition
\tau^{*}
.
By definition of
K_{j}
, for any
i,i^{\prime}\in K_{j}
, we get that
\alpha_{i,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i^{\prime},{\ell}}
. Let us denote this common codeword in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
as
f_{j,\ell}
.

Let
g_{{\ell}}\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}^{d}
be the local projection of
g
to the edge neighborhood of
{\ell}
. For every
e\in N({\ell})
that satisfies
{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)=1
, we replace the corresponding coordinate in
g_{{\ell}}
by an erasure to obtain
\overline{g}_{\ell}\in\left(\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\{\bot\}\right)^{N({\ell})}
.
The fraction of erasures in
g_{{\ell}}
is

s_{{\ell}}=\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}.

Next, we calculate,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\cdot\left(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\cdot\left(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\cdot\left(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\cdot\left(1-{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right)\right]}

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}-\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{g_{e}\neq f_{j,e}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{g_{e}\neq f_{j,e}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{g_{e}\neq f_{j,e}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{g_{e}\neq f_{j,e}\right\}}\right]}-s_{{\ell}}

\displaystyle=~\Delta(g_{{\ell}},f_{j,\ell})-s_{{\ell}}

\displaystyle=~\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell}).

Applying the
(\delta_{0},k,\varepsilon/2)
-average radius list decodability with erasures of inner code to
\overline{g}_{\ell}
and the set of codewords
\{f_{j,\ell}\}_{j\in[p]}
, we get,

\sum_{j=1}^{p}\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})~\geq~(p-1)\Big\lparen\delta_{0}-s_{{\ell}}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\rparen.

Substituting
\Delta(\overline{g}_{\ell},f_{j,\ell})=\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}
and
s_{{\ell}}=\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},e}(g,\alpha)\right]}~\geq~(p-1)\cdot\bigg\lparen\delta_{0}-\mathchoice{\underset{e\in N({\ell})}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in N({\ell})}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{[k],e}(g,\alpha)\right]}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\bigg\rparen.\qquad\hbox{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}

5.2.1
Proof of Main Theorem

We restate the main theorem and finish the proof using the above lemmas.

5.18
Restatement of
Theorem

5.13
.

Let
k_{0}\geq 1
be an integer and let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be a
code obtained using the AEL construction using
(G,{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}},{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}})
, where
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
is
(\delta_{0},k_{0},\varepsilon/2)
average-radius list decodable with erasures, and the graph
G
is a
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander.
Let
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
be an
\eta
-good
k_{0}
-tuple of pseudocodewords that satisfies the following pairwise distance property on the left:

\forall i,i^{\prime}\in[k_{0}]\text{ with }i\neq i^{\prime},\qquad\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{L}({\mathbf{Z}}_{i},{\mathbf{Z}}_{i^{\prime}})\right]}\geq\beta\,.

Further, assume that
\lambda\leq\frac{\beta}{12k_{0}^{k_{0}}}\cdot\varepsilon
and
\eta\leq\frac{\beta}{12k_{0}^{k_{0}}}\cdot\varepsilon
. Then, for any
g\in({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d})^{R}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in[k_{0}]}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}~

\displaystyle\geq~(k_{0}-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{\underset{{r}\in \displaystyle=~(k_{0}-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k_{0}]})\right]}.

Proof.

The proof, as before, is by induction on
k_{0}
. The base case,
k_{0}=1
is trivial. So we assume the statement holds upto
k-1
, and the goal is to prove it for
k_{0}=k
. Fix a partition
\tau^{*}=(K_{1},\cdots,K_{p})
as guaranteed by
5.14
. For each part
K_{j}
, we may apply the induction hypothesis to the sub-tuple defined by it as the pairwise distance property is already assumed. Using this we get,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}~

\displaystyle=~\sum_{j=1}^{p}\sum_{i\in K_{j}}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{i})\right]}

\displaystyle\geq~\sum_{j=1}^{p}\left((|K_{j}|-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}\right).

The first term is simply,

\sum_{j=1}^{p}(|K_{j}|-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)~=~(k-p)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon).
The goal is now to show that,

\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{K_{j}})\right]}~\geq~(p-1)(\delta_{0}-\varepsilon)+\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Delta_{R}(g,{\mathbf{Z}}_{[k]})\right]}.

For a fixed
j\in[p]
, we apply
Lemma

5.16
to obtain,

(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\,\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}\sim{r}}\left[\;\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\cdot{\mathsf{F}}_{K_{j},{r}}(g,{\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\,\right]}}{\mathchoice{\underset{{\ell}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\Lambda_{{\ell},\tau^{*}}({\mathbf{Z}})\right]}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{{\ell}}\left[\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\L 5.3
The final algorithm

In this subsection, we describe the final decoding algorithm. We will mainly rely on the main result of the previous subsection: that for appropriately instantiated AEL codes and for
\eta
small enough,
\eta
-good
k
-tuple of pseudocodewords satisfy the generalized Singleton bound.

5.3.1
Decoding from Distributions

Before describing our main algorithm, we argue that a simple idea based on randomized rounding can be used to extend the unique decoder of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
to decode not only from integral strings close to a codeword, but an ensemble of distributions - one for each coordinate - that is close to a codeword in average sense. It is also standard to derandomize this process via threshold rounding, which is what we describe next. We will be needing this strengthening of the unique decoder of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
as a subroutine in the main algorithm.

It will be helpful to index the codewords in
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
by integers. Let
\left\lvert{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}\right\rvert=M
, and let its codewords be
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{M}
. Recall that for any
k
-tuple of pseudocodewords
\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}
, for any
j\in[k]
and
{\ell}\in L
, the set of values

\left\{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}\right\}_{i\in[M]}

correspond to a probability distribution over
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}
. Therefore, the set of intervals

\left\{\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]},\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{{\mathbf{Z}}_{j,{\ell}}=\alpha_{i}\right\}}\right]}\right)\right\}_{m\in[M]}

partitions the interval
[0,1)
into at most
M
parts.

For every threshold
\theta\in[0,1]
:
2
2

2

As written, this involves trying uncountably many thresholds. However, we only need to try at most
M\cdot|L|
thresholds since the algorithm only depends on which intervals
\theta
belongs to. Since the number of endpoints of these intervals is at most
M\cdot|L|
in total, it suffices to try only
M\cdot|L|=M\cdot n
many distinct thresholds. This is a standard method called threshold rounding.

(i)

Construct an
f_{\theta}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{in}}^{L}
by assigning

(f_{\theta})_{{\ell}}=\alpha_{m}\iff\theta\in\left[\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}w_{{\ell},i},\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{{\ell},i}\right)

for every
{\ell}\in L
.

(ii)

Let
f_{\theta}^{*}\in\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}^{L}
defined as
(f_{\theta}^{*})_{{\ell}}=\varphi^{-1}((f_{\theta})_{{\ell}})
.

(iii)

Use the unique decoder of
{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
to find an
h^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
whose distance from
f_{\theta}^{*}
is at most
\delta_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathrm{dec}}
, if such an
h^{*}
exists. That is,
h^{*}\leftarrow\mathrm{Dec}_{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}}(f_{\theta}^{*},\delta_{\mathrm{out}}^{\mathrm{dec}})
.

(iv)

Let
h\in{{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{AEL}}}
be the codeword corresponding to
h^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\mathrm{out}}
. Return
h
.
